@@14609499 <h> Sulphur in the wind : the current crisis in South African higher education <p> File : The calls for large-scale change across South African universities can not be seen to be a new phenomenon says eNCA commentator Angelo Fick . Photo : AFP / Marco Longari <p> JOHANNESBURG - The current crisis in South African higher education is not new . The events of September 2016 , which will doubtless continue into and through the last quarter of the year , can hardly be called surprising given the way in which government officials and university managers responded on behalf of the State as a whole to the protests and demands of 2015 . <p> The protests of 2015 were also not the first of post-apartheid South Africa , and this mis-memory both underscores and encourages the continued professions of shock at the less palatable and more execrable aspects of the unfolding events , on the part of protestors or elements among them , but also on the part of the security and police actors . As students at historically Black campuses have repeatedly pointed out , they @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ throughout the post-apartheid period . <p> The difference between the protests of 2015 and the ones that preceded them was that they were happening across the campuses of formerly white universities , both the ones who had styled themselves English liberal organisations in the apartheid period -- the University of Cape Town ( UCT ) and Rhodes University ( RU ) among these -- as well as the formerly Afrikaans campuses on which Christian national education and its fascist impulses towards white supremacist science and scholarship was the order of the day throughout the last days of the ill-fated National Party regime -- Stellenbosch University ( SU ) and the University of Pretoria ( UP ) among these . <p> The calls for large-scale change across South African universities also can not be seen to be a new phenomenon . The first post-apartheid Ministry of Education headed by Sibusiso Bengu tabled discussion documents for transforming higher education in 1996 and 1997 . The process by which senior managers and academics at universities engaged these proposals led to the legislated changes in post-apartheid education which included reorganising universities , technikons , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ form and number , as well as initiating course modularisation , disciplinary unbundling and reconfiguration , and refocusing the priorities of various organisations across the variegated post-secondary education sector . <p> Part of that process was explicitly meant to undo the legacies of colonialism and apartheid in the university sector . The consequences of the Bantu Education Act ( 1953 ) and the Extension of University Education Act ( 1959 ) lingered on , and in the fortieth year since the Soweto student insurgency , they linger still . The raced inequality and inequity actively constructed by colonial and apartheid education policies -- the latter more intensively than the former -- have not been fully unmade , and not only in terms of staff and student composition , but in the very rituals and procedures by which ' business as usual ' continues , whether in curriculum content or teaching methods , whether in hierarchies of remuneration , power and its distribution inside organisations , or procedures for accountability . The toxic legacy of the past still constitutes a substantial component of the ' unfinished business ' young South Africans are @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ we , the older generation , have bequeathed them . <p> The transformation of higher education begun under Minister Bengu , which proceeded under the ministries headed by Kader Asmal and Naledi Pandor did go some way towards addressing the legacy of racist white supremacy in the organisation of post-secondary education and training . But clearly , as we were reminded over the years by staff and students on various campuses , not enough had been done . Across those merged campuses which made new universities , protests persisted : the structural and administrative changes were not thorough-going in terms of their approach to scholarship and science . <p> How universities responded to calls for change from within their own ranks go some way to explaining why we find ourselves at this juncture , in this moment of crisis in 2016 , long after the abolition of legislated white supremacy , along with the Constitutional protection against unfair discrimination on the basis of gender , sexuality , class , or creed . While the government initiated calls for and measures to undo the doleful consequences of the Christian nationalist white supremacist @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and starts , and unevenly across the entire sector , there were also calls for more fundamental ' transformation ' from a minority of academics . <p> Among the most critical of these interventions was Mahmood Mandani 's , which called for a reorientation of South African studies of Africa in relation to the history and trajectories of African studies in the other countries of post-colonial Africa in the last years of the previous millennium at the University of Cape Town offered a pivotal moment to begin what would later be called ' decolonising the curriculum ' . The debate he and Amina Mama engaged in -- against Wilmot James , the Dean of Humanities at the time -- in the Centre for African Studies at UCT broadened the discussion beyond the curriculum of specific course , ' Introduction to Africa ' , to wider questions about the political economy of post-apartheid scientific inquiry in universities . Saul Dubow 's A Commonwealth of Knowledge ( 2006 ) gives additional insight into the shaping of whiteness in and by the South African colonial , apartheid , and post-apartheid university and research sectors @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ economic hardships faced by a substantial proportion of students across post-secondary education , the political violations of institutional and tolerated racism , sexism , and homophobia too often remained part of post-millennial post-apartheid education for another generation of young people . The depths of trauma in many young women 's experience of higher education in a femicidal , misogynistic society in which rape functions as Pumla Gqola demonstrates in Rape : A South African Nightmare ( 2015 ) , may perhaps never be fully fathomed by many of us . The stories students have been telling , and still tell , of the experiences that they are still made to have ought to shame those of us who value post-apartheid South African freedom . It also ought to make us angry ; and thus , it ought to give us pause to consider the rage students express , to introspect and prospect for its causes . <p> Only then can we begin to work towards addressing the symptoms in this university crisis some among us seem so focused on -- the violence , the ill-discipline and property destruction -- by dealing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a long time , in our reading of and response to the crime against humanity which was apartheid South Africa , many of us held that there could be no normality in an abnormal society . We built sports and cultural boycotts on this principle . We insisted there be transformation of the society as a whole before there could be normal sports . We demanded that the doors of learning and culture be open , meaning to all , not only to those who could pay . Why would we imagine the principle does not still apply ? We are far from a normal society . <p> In fact , much of what is currently happening recall for those of us who were alive and paying critical attention to the world around us , scenes from the late 1980s , during apartheid 's last spasm of violence . Men in the carapace of armoured uniform firing at young bodies in flight , stones flung by young bodies in revolt at the edifices of power ( or sites they saw as such ) , the criminalisation of political protest , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the protests to proceed with the business they were on campus for : these are all familiar scenes and postures . That some of us have to live through them a second time -- 1986 , 2016 ; a neat generation in between -- feels not only unfair , but accursed . But , again , as the young people at TUT and the University of Zululand remind us : these scenes are only a return for some us ; for them , these scenes have been playing out regularly throughout the post-1994 settlement . <p> The texture of the freedom some of us have had , and are now keen to smooth out , is velvet compared to the crude hessian sacking with which others have had to make do . Uncomfortable as it may be , and disempowering as it may feel , it may be important for those of us who have power -- political , economic , social -- to listen to the protestors , and not only the ones demanding free higher education , and to think about how we can address both the symptoms of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ are , after the all , the ones who made the world they now live in . We owe them , and ourselves , something other than a return to old habits of repression and securitisation . <p> And we need not wait for history or History to judge us ; we can judge ourselves , now , in this moment , by our own words and promises . In August 2013 the Ministry of Higher Education , headed by Blade Nzimande , published the Report of the Working Group on Free University Education for the Poor in South Africa . In October 2013 , the same ministry published a Report of the Ministerial Committee for the Review of the Funding of Universities . Both reports were available before the last election which returned Mr Nzimande 's party with 62% . Both reports date from a period before the current economic trouble South Africa finds itself in in the wake of events from the last quarter of 2015 . We have had three ' State of the Nation ' addresses by President Jacob Zuma , and three Budget speeches by two @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ pre-date the ANC 's NGC in 2015 , or the SACP and COSATU conferences of last year . <p> We owe it to ourselves to be honest about how we got here , the road marked by political indecision and expedient revision . We should also not pretend that the changes we have been telling ourselves are necessary have to be put off for being unaffordable . Certainly not at a time when public money is being dedicated to saving public enterprises which have been miserably managed to the point where they are mounting additional and obscene figures onto their already onerous debt , which is essentially our debt , public debt . <p> We are not a poor country : we are merely a country in which the unequal distribution of resources at the heart of the inequality no longer borders on inhumanity . Free public education is not a mistake of some earlier vision of future freedom which must now be delegitimised and renounced . It is crucial to the very dreams we foster in this present about the no-longer distant future . In addition to the other problems @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of climate change , water insecurity , food insecurity , pollution , and the conflicts which will result from these , and the waves of refugees and migrants those will in turn result in -- we are not also burden them with the financial debt of paying for an inadequate , mediocre education which barely helps them accommodate to the present , and certainly will not equip them to deal with the challenges of the future . 
@@14616659 <h> Hail the Victorious Igo Venter . Although he almost loses his balance . <p> Ian von Memerty is a Zimbabwean-born South African entertainer , actor , singer , musician , writer , director and television presenter . See his full biography here . <p> 30 Sep 2016 01:00 ( South Africa ) <p> Is it tautologically possible to describe an article about environmental disaster as a breath of fresh air ? It has to be , because that is exactly what Igo Venter 's well-researched article last Tuesday was . A meticulous and succinct list of DoomsDay prophecies about the environment that have been endlessly regurgitated by the media , until they became the gospel truth for millions of people . And all of these predictions -- WRONG ! <p> In his article he highlights two intrinsic flaws in the way the worldwide environmental movements have presented their case(s) . <p> First , they stated theoretical , non-verifiable worst-case scenarios as absolute scientific facts -- inevitable and unstoppable . Since I was a child I have lived with the ongoing prediction that oil was about to run @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and gas available today than ever before ; that the world economy would collapse TOMORROW under a never ending population increase -- when despite the fact we have passed the 7-billion mark , population growth has already started to flatten out , and will stop growing completely in about 40 years before beginning a slow shrinkage . Starvation and famine are on the decrease , not increasingly ravaging the world as predicted -- and they affect a smaller percentage of the world with every year that passes . <p> By over-hyping a potential problem " environmentalists " have painted themselves into a corner -- they are the boy who cried wolf . Like those false prophets who give a definite date for the world 's end , we keep passing predictions in remarkably good shape . And so we distrust everything they say because the hysteria and overstatement drowns out the core of possible sense that they are preaching . <p> The second mistake they have made is not to place current facts and trends with-in a long-term historical context . When you are dealing with the environment to get a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to think in terms of centuries and millennia , not decades . Historically we are enjoying a warm spell in the middle of an ice age , although we had a mini " Ice Age " in the 16-18th centuries . Temperatures have fluctuated and we have been both hotter and colder than we are now ; the world has also been both drier and wetter . <p> The cradle of civilisation and the first bread basket of history , the Middle East , is now a dirty brown smudge on the global map . Climate change is ongoing , constant and the causes are complex and interlinked . ( If you think I am just blindly trusting Igo Venter , you should read Abundance by Peter Diamandis and Steven Kotler , another meticulously researched and eminently readable book ) . <p> The environmentalists have become ever-more strident to compensate for the fact that they are increasingly mistrusted because of a series of sensationalist and inaccurate predictions . In short , they have been the Donald Trump of the Natural World -- " the world is going to hell and we @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ suffer a catastrophe " . <p> However , I think that Igo Venter , in his article , falls into the same sand trap that the environmentalists do but from the opposite side , ( but his fall is not nearly as hard or as far as the environmentalists ' ) . Because there are certain scientific facts about mankind 's effect on the environment that are not in dispute . Since the Industrial Revolution began and fossil fuels became our primary source of energy , carbon levels in the atmosphere have risen at an alarming rate beyond anything yet recorded in millions of years . In the early 1990s CFCs had burnt a hole in the ozone layer , which would have been potentially disastrous if not dealt with . Deforestation is a real ongoing problem in terms of habitat loss , extinction of species , erosion etc . And there are species that have become extinct due to habitat loss or uncontrolled hunting and poaching . <p> If the world had not acknowledged that CFCs had to be eradicated , and criminalised the use of CFCs , the hole @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and no one has ever said that would have been a good thing . If China had not introduced legislation to protect the natural habitat of the Giant Panda , that species would not recently have been taken off the " endangered " list . If the last remaining 300 American Bison ( 1,900 ) had not been saved , then reintroduced into protected areas , we would not have close to half a million of these magnificent beasts today . <p> In other words , by failing to give perspective , just as environmental disaster predictors do , Venter encourages us to ignore facts . We dare not do this ; otherwise carbon levels will continue to rise and we do n't know what all the consequences will be , except that we will have interfered with the earth 's " natural cycle " , and that is a dangerous game to play . Without stopping deforestation , and controlling poaching , we could lose some of the earth 's biological diversity -- and that would be tragic and expensive . <p> Both sides need to use calm , cool @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ leave that to the politicians -- they come and go fast enough ( even Robert Mugabe will go soon ! Simple laws of nature will triumph ) . <p> I do understand why both sides get hysterical -- bad news captures the human imagination . Studies have proven it could well be genetic . The more suspicious our ancestors were , the more likely they were to survive . If you constantly suspected the world of wanting to do you harm you were less likely to suffer a deadly surprise attack by a carnivore , or to get a spear in the back , or on returning to your cave to find your woman had been dragged off by some competing horny Neanderthal . ( Please excuse the implied misogyny but there seems to be little anthropological evidence that female Neanderthals were abducting men for procreative purposes -- possibly because even then females were 10 steps ahead of us males and had worked out that it really was n't worth the effort . ) . <p> My point is that as a race we have outpaced our evolutionary development in the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ very careful how we present facts . Our inbuilt negative protection mechanism needs time to adapt to the new world , which is the cumulative effect of our combined brilliance . Hysteria and over-the-top predictions are not useful or truthful , but it is equally unhelpful and destructive to ignore what we are doing to the world around us . <p> But for all my quibbles , Igo Venter is one of those rare and much needed voices that are encouraging us to grow up , look at the facts , and not listen to the hype . And that is not only good for the environment in general , but is excellent for our individual lives in particular . Steel companies are reeling under low prices for finished steel and merchant sale from their captive coal mines will assist their cash flow and ease pressures on margins . <p> As for thermal power companies , their plant load factor is down to levels of 59% , compared with 70% to 75% about a year ago , owing to falling demand for electricity . This , in turn , has resulted @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ production from captive mines . <p> However , the government will make it mandatory that all coal from captive mines made available on a merchant sale basis , is suitably washed or beneficiated at pitheads in line with government 's focus on ' clean coal energy ' . <p> Permitting merchant sale of coal from captive mines is also expected to open up a window for leaseholders to leverage the asset and woo fresh investment , the official said . <p> For example , steel companies , stressed by outstanding debts , would have the option of hiving off the captive mine into a separate entity and inviting foreign direct investment into the coal mine asset , the official said . <p> However , he was quick to add a caveat that given the depressed international coal business environment and concerns over climate change in developed economies , appetite for investing in coal assets was not too high either . <p> A back of envelop calculation indicates that at current average aggregate production from captive coal mines and average consumption of the dry fuel by respective user industries , an estimated @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the open market . <p> While the changing the rules governing the production and sale of coal from captive mines is expected to have a salutary impact on leaseholders burdened with excess production , no clarity is available as to how government will tackle the issue of more coal flowing into an oversupplied market . <p> At the moment , Coal India Limited is saddled with pithead stocks of 42-million tons and another estimated 20-million tons are stockpiled at thermal power plants . 
@@14623281 <p> The project provides accessible potable water to more than 400 people in five villages , most of them women and children , the UNFCCC secretariat said in a press release . <p> Website screenshot . Credit : www.darsihmad.org <p> Dar Si Hmad , a women-led NGO in Morocco , designed and installed what is now the world 's largest operational fogwater harvesting system , the UNFCCC said . <p> " It is an innovative solution to persistent water stress where fog is abundant , a technique inspired from ancient water practices , " it noted . <p> The project is a successful model of a locally-driven , participatory climate change adaptation initiative , providing an environmentally friendly water source to combat the effects of desertification , the UNFCCC said . <p> Each of the 13 winning activities touches on one of Momentum for Change 's three focus areas : women for results , financing for climate friendly investment and ICT solutions . <p> All 13 will be showcased at a series of special events during the UN Climate Change Conference in Marrakech , Morocco in November . 
@@14629765 <h> Related Links <p> Nape Town - Southern Africa has suffered through one of the worst droughts in decades and now small farmers face a long hungry season with growing food aid needs until the next harvest . <p> Given the growing uncertainty of rainfall in this region , many are turning to irrigation as a key strategy for securing future harvests . The problem , however , is that male and female farmers have deeply unequal rights to critical resources , including water . <p> Uneven access to water also complicates assumptions about the ability of commercial farming to deliver household food security in the African context . <p> The 2015-16 drought was the worst to hit Southern Africa in 35 years , leaving an estimated 32 million people in the region food insecure . This hunger will only deepen until March , 2017 when new harvests normally arrive . <h> Not all women farmers are equal <p> While women grow the majority of food in this region , public programmes and policies are skewed towards providing water for male livelihood activities . This bias undermines female @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the food security of the region . <p> In the semi-arid , southern African country of Botswana , women rely heavily on irrigation to grow vegetables during the long dry season . These crops are produced for home consumption and to generate needed income . <p> While the government has provided much needed support for this activity in recent years , they have been disappointed with the results , even considering it an utter failure in some cases . <p> What these appraisals overlook is the critical role that water access plays in the success of a women 's vegetable production operation , and the overall way in which women 's farming activities are marginalised in the country . <p> In fact , differences in water access among female vegetable farmers in Botswana had a huge influence on whether or not commercial horticulture helps improve household food security . <p> Wealthy female farmers have the financial resources , or access to credit , to invest in boreholes . For these women , increasing commercialisation of vegetable production ( or selling a larger share of their crop ) leads to clear gains @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ female gardeners who ca n't afford a well , and water their gardens with purchased tap water . From them , there are only modest food security gains that accompany increasing commercial gardening . <p> Still others get their water from a river , where the costs are much lower , and they do very well when they focus on feeding their families . Interestingly , this last group sees a decline in household food security when they begin selling more and consuming less of the vegetables they grow . <p> Why the difference in these outcomes ? Wealthier female farmers with land can afford the upfront costs of a borehole , and often have the necessary connections to successfully engage with the market , allowing them to cover the costs of their initial investment and go on to make a reasonable profit that enhances their food security . <p> Those with lower water costs , the river water users , face less pressure to commercialise to cover costs , and do very well directly feeding their families rather that attempting to negotiate the vagaries of the marketplace . <p> Tap @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ commercialise to cover the cost of the water they are using , yet do not always have the connections to negotiate the marketplace , leading to modest income gains and minimal food security benefits . <h> Unequal resource access <p> These results bring to light at least two important issues related to food security , agriculture and resource access in Sub-Saharan Africa . <p> First , while increasing commercialisation of agriculture in Sub-Saharan Africa has been championed as a pathway to improved food security , this view neglects how access to resources is a complicating factor . <p> Growing more crops for the market may make sense for some , namely wealthy farmers who make the investments to secure water access and are commercially savvy . In other cases , selling more crops on the market may actually undermine food security . <p> Second , in Botswana there is a significant gender component to water access . The historical significance of the cattle industry , which is dominated by men , has meant that government agencies and programmes support the development of water sources for cattle to the exclusion of other @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ priority to male livelihoods forces female horticulturalists to either find creative ways to negotiate these constraints or to see their gardens flounder and household food security benefits dwindle . <p> Recognising the importance of affordable access to water for agricultural success is important in Botswana and Sub-Saharan Africa more broadly . <p> In Botswana it means that government programmes supporting women 's gardening activities will not be successful unless they recognise and address female farmers ' unequal access to water . <p> More broadly , the international development community , which has focused on improving food security through commercial agriculture , must acknowledge the importance of unequal resource access . <p> Commercialisation of agriculture often does not lead to welfare improvements if the power dynamics involved in resource access are not recognised and addressed . In fact , greater commercialisation may increase vulnerability and food insecurity where inequitable access persists . <p> Addressing women 's inequitable access to water resources will only become more important in Southern Africa as the region grapples with climate change , increasing rainfall variability and growing need for irrigation . <p> Rachel Fehr recently completed her @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and economics , with a concentration in African Studies .
